For each element the products are\n// accumulated in the same k order as the sequential reference.\n__global__ void __launch_bounds__(THREADS)\nmatmulKernel(const double* __restrict__ A, const double* __restrict__ B,\n             double* __restrict__ C, const size_t rows, const size_t N) {\n    __shared__ double As[BK][BM + 1];\n    __shared__ double Bs[BK][BN];\n\n    const int tx = threadIdx.x;\n    const int ty = threadIdx.y;\n    const int tid = ty * TX + tx;\n    const size_t rowBase = static_cast<size_t>(blockIdx.y) * BM;\n    const size_t colBase = static_cast<size_t>(blockIdx.x) * BN;\n\n    double acc[TM][TN];\n#pragma unroll\n    for (int i = 0; i < TM; ++i)\n#pragma unroll\n        for (int j = 0; j < TN; ++j) acc[i][j] = 0.0;\n\n    for (size_t k0 = 0; k0 < N; k0 += BK) {\n        // Load A tile (BM x BK), stored transposed: As[k][m]\n#pragma unroll\n        for (int l = 0; l < (BM * BK) / THREADS; ++l) {\n            const int idx = tid + l * THREADS;\n            const int m = idx / BK;\n            const int k = idx % BK;\n            const size_t gr = rowBase + m;\n            const size_t gk = k0 + k;\n            As[k][m] = (gr < rows && gk < N) ? Small problems use fewer GPUs, at least 256 rows per GPU.\n- Each GPU runs its own copy-in, compute and copy-back, all at the same time.\n\n**Matching the original's rounding exactly**\nI looked at the machine code of the original build. The compiler processes the `k` loop in pairs, rounding each product before adding it. When N is odd, it fuses the last multiply and add into one instruction. The GPU kernel reproduces exactly that, which is why the hashes match for both odd and even N.\n\n**Decision for you:** this exact matching roughly halves GPU speed, because the 3090 is slow at double precision and separate multiply-add takes twice as many operations. Fusing every multiply-add would be about 1.9× faster and slightly more accurate. It still passes validation, but the hash differs from the original whenever N is not a power of two.
